Memo Artscape Newsletter MinnPost Timberwolves Newsletter Careers Festival About Us Open dropdown menu Inside MinnPost Open Search Search for: Search Topics State Government Metro Greater Minnesota Elections Washington Mental Health &#038; Addiction Photography Sports Documenters Fact Briefs Posted in Politics & Policy The 29 legislative races to watch in Minnesota, updated by Tom Nehil , Briana Bierschbach and Greta Kaul 11/03/2016 11/03/2016 Share this: Email a link to a friend (Opens in new window) Email Share on Facebook (Opens in new window) Facebook Share on Bluesky (Opens in new window) Bluesky Share on X (Opens in new window) X Print (Opens in new window) Print [raw shortcodes=1] It’s less than a week from Election Day in Minnesota, and voters are hearing all about shadowy outside influences, the state’s health insurance exchange and — of course — the presidential race. Those issues have dominated a strange campaign season in Minnesota, an election in which all 201 legislators are on the ballot but with no major statewide candidate anchoring the ticket. Instead, the presidential contest between Donald Trump and Hillary Clinton is the single big race, a pairing that’s had complicated implications for the battle to control the Minnesota Legislature. In October, Republican House Speaker Kurt Daudt denounced Trump after an “Access Hollywood” tape leaked of the Republican nominee saying he inappropriately grabbed and groped women. Republican legislative candidates in suburban swing districts are trying to avoid talking about Trump altogether, where he polls far behind Clinton. Likewise, out in rural Minnesota, Democrats are trying to talk about anything but Clinton, who is far less popular in the far-flung regions of the state than she is in the Twin Cities. It hasn’t been easy for local legislative candidates to steer the conversation away from the presidential race to local issues, but that hasn’t stopped them from trying. Glossy mailers, radio and television ads have hit Republicans for cutting services too deeply, while others have attacked Democrats for taxing and spending too much. Both sides have tried to tie the other to mysterious outside interests, like “Twin Cities liberals” and “corporate” overlords. The race for the Legislature also got an October surprise: Premiums on the individual marketplace through the state’s health insurance exchange, MNsure, went up between 50 and 67 percent. Responding to the news, DFL Gov. Mark Dayton said the Affordable Care Act was “no longer” affordable for many people, a quote that’s appeared in Republican mailers and television ads across the state in the final stretch of the campaign. The stakes are high: Democrats control the Senate and the governor’s office and are hoping to win back the House, which would give them complete control of government for the next two years. Republicans, who took over the state House in 2014, are fighting to maintain control of the chamber and win back a majority in the Senate. And though all 201 seats across the state are on the ballot, there are only a handful of battleground races in the suburbs and Greater Minnesota that are truly competitive — and provide any real opportunity for the parties to make gains. In the House, Republicans hold a 73-61 majority, meaning Democrats need to pick up at least seven seats to take back control. Republicans see a few pickup opportunities in open seats from Rochester to Minnetonka, where Democrats now in office are retiring, but they are also playing defense on seats they picked up in 2014, like those in St. Cloud and Willmar, as well as several key suburban swing seats. Likewise, Democrats want to capitalize on a few open Republican-held seats in Apple Valley and Red Wing, and they’ve set up a handful of rematches and competitive races in Greater Minnesota to try and win back a majority. In the Senate, Democrats hold a 39-28 majority, meaning Republicans need to pick up at least six seats to win back control. So the GOP is focusing on seats that normally lean conservative but that the DFL managed to pick up in 2012, as well as a few rural districts that Democrats have held for years. Democrats, meanwhile, hope to expand their majority by picking up a seat in an open race in St. Cloud and a competitive contest in Eden Prairie. In an effort to sort this all out, MinnPost looked at election data and talked to campaign operatives in both parties to compile a list of 29 races — 17 in the House and 12 in the Senate — that will decide control of the Legislature this fall. All 29 races to watch are displayed below. To more easily compare, contrast and explore the races, however, you can click the "Show only" links to toggle the various filters on and off. Note: Spending data combines cash expenditures by campaigns and by independent expenditure groups. [/raw][raw shortcodes=1] Show only: Districts to Watch Senate House DFL controlled GOP controlled Obama won Romney won Open Seat Metro Greater MN Show All Clear State Senate District 1 District to Watch Incumbent: None &#8211; open seat Candidates: Kip Fontaine (DFL) Mark Johnson (R) Part of the recipe for Democrats long control of the state Senate is a handful of popular incumbents who’ve managed to hold on to rural districts that naturally lean conservative. DFL Sen. LeRoy Stumpf held his Republican-leaning northern Minnesota Senate seat since 1982, but his retirement puts it back in play this fall. Republicans endorsed Mark Johnson, an attorney from East Grand Forks. Democrats initially liked their chances with endorsed-candidate Kip Fontaine, a public defender from Red Lake Falls, but DFL outside resources have dried up in that race, which suggests the race is likely to be a pickup for Republicans. Map & Total spending Spent to help Democrats $9,212.27 Spent to help Republicans $71,861.67 22nd highest spending of all Senate districts. Past election results This district, 2012: Leroy Stumpf (DFL) 60.59% Steve Nordhagen (R) 39.33% State House District 1A, 2014: Dan Fabian (R) 67.09% Bruce Patterson (DFL) 32.82% State House District 1B, 2014: Debra (Deb) Kiel (R) 55.61% Eric Bergeson (DFL) 44.34% President, 2012: Romney (R) 53.04% Obama (D) 44.45% State Senate District 2 District to Watch Incumbent: Rod Skoe (DFL) (First elected: 2002) Challengers: Paul Utke (R) DFL Sen. Rod Skoe’s district leans conservative, but he’s managed to hold on to the seat for three terms. This year, Republican outside spending groups are targeting him with a fervor that’s unusual for the rural north-central Minnesota Senate district. But picking up conservative rural seats like Skoe’s is part of the Republican strategy to regain control of the upper chamber. Skoe’s opponent is Paul Utke, an insurance agent who serves on the Park Rapids City Council. Republicans also hope anti-Clinton sentiment in the area will help them win. Republican Sen. John Pederson is retiring after two terms, and his party recruited Jerry Relph, a small business owner in the district with a similar profile as Pederson. Democrats have recruited Dan Wolgamott, a young real estate agent who made an unsuccessful bid for the more conservative part of the district in the House last cycle. Lyle Koenen has one of the most conservative voting records of any member of his caucus, and for good reason: His district is one of the most Republican-leaning districts still held by Democrats. Koenen is a lifelong resident of Chippewa County and works as milk contract hauler. He was first elected to the Senate in 2012 after serving a decade in the state House, so he’s well known in the area. But outside spending groups are taking their best shot at unseating him this year, and Republicans have put forward Andrew Lang, a Minnesota National Guard officer and pilot from Olivia. Dahle (DFL) (First elected: 2008) Challengers: Rich Draheim (R) This state Senate seat represents a classic swing district, meaning it’s competitive every cycle, no matter who’s at the top of the ticket. That’s because the district includes Northfield, which leans Democratic, but also many small towns and townships, which tend lean Republican. That’s true again this cycle, as incumbent DFL Rep. Kevin Dahle defends his seat against Republican Rich Draheim, a relator in the area. Dahle (DFL) 50.02% Mike Dudley (R) 49.85% State House District 20A, 2014: Bob Vogel (R) 64.29% Thomas Lofgren (DFL) 35.52% State House District 20B, 2014: David Bly (DFL) 57.97% Dan Matejcek (R) 41.8% President, 2012: Romney (R) 50.43% Obama (D) 47.12% State Senate District 21 District to Watch Incumbent: Matt Schmit (DFL) (First elected: 2012) Challengers: Mike Goggin (R) Matt Schmit has a reputation as a hard campaigner in his Red Wing-area Senate district, but he’s on the ballot for the first time since he was first elected in 2012, a good year for Democrats. The district has swung back and forth over the years, and Republicans have recruited Mike Goggins, a union member who works at a power plant in the district. The Goggin name is strong in the area, according to Republicans, as his father used to run the Red Wing Shoe Company. Terri Bonoff is on to bigger things this election cycle, making a run at the 3rd Congressional District and incumbent GOP U.S. Rep. Erik Paulsen. That leaves open this competitive Minnetonka and Plymouth-area seat, which Bonoff first won in a special election in 2005. She held on to the swing seat over the years with a moderate tone and business-friendly positions on everything from tax increases to the minimum wage. To replace Bonoff, Democrats recruited Deb Calvert, a former staffer for Sen. Amy Klobuchar who now works in the dean’s office at Mitchell Hamline School of Law. But Republicans are excited about their candidate, too: Paul Anderson, a well-known Republican activist who previously served as a deputy chief of staff for former Gov. Tim Pawlenty. As of now, this race on track to be the most expensive single race for the Legislature this year, with more than $700,000 spent by mid October. Jim Knoblach is in a close race that could cost House Republicans their chief budget guru. Zachary Dorholt, is running again, and this year he’ll have boosted voter turnout at his back, especially in a town with a large public university. Expect Republicans to work hard to defend Knoblach, making this one of the most-watched — and the single most expensive — races in the House this cycle. Ron Erhardt versus Republican challenger Dario Anselmo. In 2014, Anselmo came within 3 points of knocking off Erhardt. Conventional wisdom would suggest the incumbent will hold onto his seat in presidential year, when turnout is higher, but Republicans have launched an effective ad campaign against him. They’ve spent tens of thousands of dollars on a television ad and mailers featuring a woman who was part of an effort in 2015 to expand the state’s sexual assault laws to include bodily fluids after a co-worker ejaculated into her coffee cup. While legislators debated the issue on the House floor, Erhardt joked and licked a coffee cup as he addressed other lawmakers. If you have questions, email feedback@minnpost.com . — The Editors The 29 legislative races to watch in Minnesota, updated by Tom Nehil, Briana Bierschbach and Greta Kaul, MinnPost November 3, 2016 &lt;h1&gt;The 29 legislative races to watch in Minnesota, updated&lt;/h1&gt; &lt;p class="byline"&gt;by Tom Nehil, Briana Bierschbach and Greta Kaul, MinnPost &lt;br /&gt;November 3, 2016&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;[raw shortcodes=1]&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; body .node-unpublished {background-repeat: no-repeat;}&lt;br /&gt; .hidden {display: none;}&lt;br /&gt; .filter-links li a {padding:5px; font-size: .8em;}&lt;br /&gt; .filtering, .filtering a {color: #fff!important; background-color:#0793AB; border-radius: 5px;}&lt;br /&gt; .mp .election {padding: 1em; border-bottom: 1px solid #ccc;}&lt;br /&gt; .mp .map-link {margin: 0 0 1em;}&lt;br /&gt; .mp #total-contests {padding-bottom: 1em; border-bottom: 1px solid #ccc;}&lt;br /&gt; .text-right {text-align: right;}&lt;br /&gt; .search-box {width: 100%; margin: 0 auto 1em; text-align: center}&lt;br /&gt; .mp .form-input-group {width:80%; margin: 0 auto;}&lt;br /&gt; .mp .map-container {width: 100%; text-align: center; margin-bottom:1em;}&lt;br /&gt; .mp .to-watch {&lt;br /&gt; border-radius: 5px;&lt;br /&gt; padding:.25em 1em;&lt;br /&gt; margin-bottom: 1em;&lt;br /&gt; background-color: #0793AB;&lt;br /&gt; color: #fff;&lt;br /&gt; display: inline-block;&lt;br /&gt; }&lt;br /&gt; #text_search {width: 100%;}&lt;br /&gt; #total-contests {text-align: center;}&lt;/p&gt; &lt;div class="mp"&gt; &lt;div class="text-block large"&gt; &lt;p&gt;It’s less than a week from Election Day in Minnesota, and voters are hearing all about shadowy outside influences, the state’s health insurance exchange and —&nbsp;of course —&nbsp;the presidential race.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;Those issues have dominated a strange campaign season in Minnesota, an election in which all 201 legislators are on the ballot but with no major statewide candidate anchoring the ticket. Instead, the presidential contest between Donald Trump and Hillary Clinton is the single big race, a pairing that’s had complicated implications for the battle to control the Minnesota Legislature.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;In October, Republican House Speaker Kurt Daudt denounced Trump after an “Access Hollywood” tape leaked of the Republican nominee saying he inappropriately grabbed and groped women. Republican legislative candidates in suburban swing districts are trying to avoid talking about Trump altogether, where he polls far behind Clinton. Likewise, out in rural Minnesota, Democrats are trying to talk about anything but Clinton, who is far less popular in the far-flung regions of the state than she is in the Twin Cities.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;It hasn’t been easy for local legislative candidates to steer the conversation away from the presidential race to local issues, but that hasn’t stopped them from trying. Glossy mailers, radio and television ads have hit Republicans for cutting services too deeply, while others have attacked Democrats for taxing and spending too much. Both sides have tried to tie the other to mysterious outside interests, like “Twin Cities liberals” and “corporate” overlords.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;The race for the Legislature also got an October surprise: Premiums on the individual marketplace through the state’s health insurance exchange, MNsure, went up between 50 and 67 percent. Responding to the news, DFL Gov. Mark Dayton said the Affordable Care Act was “no longer” affordable for many people, a quote that’s appeared in Republican mailers and television ads across the state in the final stretch of the campaign.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;The stakes are high: Democrats control the Senate and the governor’s office and are hoping to win back the House, which would give them complete control of government for the next two years. Republicans, who took over the state House in 2014, are fighting to maintain control of the chamber and win back a majority in the Senate. And though all 201 seats across the state are on the ballot, there are only a handful of battleground races in the suburbs and Greater Minnesota that are truly competitive — and provide any real opportunity for the parties to make gains.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;In the House, Republicans hold a 73-61 majority, meaning Democrats need to pick up at least seven seats to take back control. Republicans see a few pickup opportunities in open seats from Rochester to Minnetonka, where Democrats now in office are retiring, but they are also playing defense on seats they picked up in 2014, like those in St. Cloud and Willmar, as well as several key suburban swing seats. Likewise, Democrats want to capitalize on a few open Republican-held seats in Apple Valley and Red Wing, and they’ve set up a handful of rematches and competitive races in Greater Minnesota to try and win back a majority.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;In the Senate, Democrats hold a 39-28 majority, meaning Republicans need to pick up at least six seats to win back control. So the GOP is focusing on seats that normally lean conservative but that the DFL managed to pick up in 2012, as well as a few rural districts that Democrats have held for years. Democrats, meanwhile, hope to expand their majority by picking up a seat in an open race in St. Cloud and a competitive contest in Eden Prairie.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;In an effort to sort this all out, MinnPost looked at election data and talked to campaign operatives in both parties to compile a list of 29 races — 17 in the House and 12 in the Senate — that will decide control of the Legislature this fall.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;All 29 races to watch are displayed below. To more easily compare, contrast and explore the races, however, you can click the "Show only" links to toggle the various filters on and off.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Note: Spending data combines cash expenditures by campaigns and by independent expenditure groups.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="#" data-filter="senate"&gt;Senate&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="#" data-filter="house"&gt;House&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="#" data-filter="dfl-control"&gt;DFL controlled&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="#" data-filter="gop-control"&gt;GOP controlled&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="#" data-filter="obama-won"&gt;Obama won&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="#" data-filter="romney-won"&gt;Romney won&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="#" data-filter="open-seat"&gt;Open Seat&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="#" data-filter="metro"&gt;Metro&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="#" data-filter="greatermn"&gt;Greater MN&lt;/a&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="#" data-filter="reset"&gt;Show All&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="search-box"&gt; &lt;div class="form-input-group"&gt; &lt;div class="button-group"&gt; &lt;button type="reset" id="search-reset" class="button info"&gt;Clear&lt;/button&gt; &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div id="total-contests"&gt; &lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="election greatermn senate featured dfl-control open-seat romney-won"&gt; &lt;div class="row"&gt; &lt;h4&gt;State Senate District 1&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li&gt;None - open seat&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Candidates:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Kip Fontaine (DFL)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Mark Johnson (R)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;Part of the recipe for Democrats long control of the state Senate is a handful of popular incumbents who’ve managed to hold on to rural districts that naturally lean conservative. DFL Sen. LeRoy Stumpf held his Republican-leaning northern Minnesota Senate seat since 1982, but his retirement puts it back in play this fall. Republicans endorsed Mark Johnson, an attorney from East Grand Forks. Democrats initially liked their chances with endorsed-candidate Kip Fontaine, a public defender from Red Lake Falls, but DFL outside resources have dried up in that race, which suggests the race is likely to be a pickup for Republicans.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;h4&gt;State Senate District 2&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Rod Skoe (DFL)&lt;/span&gt; (First elected: 2002)&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Challengers:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Paul Utke (R)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;DFL Sen. Rod Skoe’s district leans conservative, but he’s managed to hold on to the seat for three terms. This year, Republican outside spending groups are targeting him with a fervor that’s unusual for the rural north-central Minnesota Senate district. But picking up conservative rural seats like Skoe’s is part of the Republican strategy to regain control of the upper chamber. Skoe’s opponent is Paul Utke, an insurance agent who serves on the Park Rapids City Council. Republicans also hope anti-Clinton sentiment in the area will help them win.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="component-label"&gt;Map &amp; &lt;h4&gt;State Senate District 14&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li&gt;None - open seat&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Candidates:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Dan Wolgamott (DFL)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Jerry Relph (R)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;St. Cloud residents have been inundated with campaign ads and mailers this fall with an open seat in the Senate. Republican Sen. John Pederson is retiring after two terms, and his party recruited Jerry Relph, a small business owner in the district with a similar profile as Pederson. Democrats have recruited Dan Wolgamott, a young real estate agent who made an unsuccessful bid for the more conservative part of the district in the House last cycle.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;h4&gt;State Senate District 17&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Lyle Koenen (DFL)&lt;/span&gt; (First elected: 2012)&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Challengers:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Andrew Lang (R)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;DFL Sen. Lyle Koenen has one of the most conservative voting records of any member of his caucus, and for good reason: His district is one of the most Republican-leaning districts still held by Democrats. Koenen is a lifelong resident of Chippewa County and works as milk contract hauler. He was first elected to the Senate in 2012 after serving a decade in the state House, so he’s well known in the area. But outside spending groups are taking their best shot at unseating him this year, and Republicans have put forward Andrew Lang, a Minnesota National Guard officer and pilot from Olivia.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;h4&gt;State Senate District 20&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Kevin L. Dahle (DFL)&lt;/span&gt; (First elected: 2008)&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Challengers:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Rich Draheim (R)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;This state Senate seat represents a classic swing district, meaning it’s competitive every cycle, no matter who’s at the top of the ticket. That’s because the district includes Northfield, which leans Democratic, but also many small towns and townships, which tend lean Republican. That’s true again this cycle, as incumbent DFL Rep. Kevin Dahle defends his seat against Republican Rich Draheim, a relator in the area.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="component-label"&gt;Map &amp; Total spending&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-container"&gt; &lt;div id="map-20" class="map" style="height:400px;width:95%"&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;table&gt; &lt;tbody&gt; &lt;tr&gt; &lt;td&gt;Spent to help Democrats&lt;/td&gt; &lt;td class="text-right"&gt;$400,783.22&lt;/td&gt; &lt;/tr&gt; &lt;tr&gt; &lt;td&gt;Spent to help Republicans&lt;/td&gt; &lt;td class="text-right"&gt;$152,495.26&lt;/td&gt; &lt;/tr&gt; &lt;/tbody&gt; &lt;/table&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;&lt;small&gt;7th highest spending of all Senate districts.&lt;/small&gt;&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="past-result column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="component-label"&gt;Past election results&lt;/div&gt; &lt;table&gt; &lt;tbody&gt; &lt;tr&gt; &lt;td colspan="2"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;This district, 2012:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; &lt;/tr&gt; &lt;tr&gt; &lt;td&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Kevin L. &lt;h4&gt;State Senate District 21&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Matt Schmit (DFL)&lt;/span&gt; (First elected: 2012)&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Challengers:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Mike Goggin (R)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;Matt Schmit has a reputation as a hard campaigner in his Red Wing-area Senate district, but he’s on the ballot for the first time since he was first elected in 2012, a good year for Democrats. The district has swung back and forth over the years, and Republicans have recruited Mike Goggins, a union member who works at a power plant in the district. The Goggin name is strong in the area, according to Republicans, as his father used to run the Red Wing Shoe Company.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;John Jasinski is one of several Republican recruits this cycle in the so-called “Mayor’s Club.” Jasinski is the two-term Mayor of Faribault and a former city council member, and Republicans are excited about his popularity in the district’s biggest population center. But Jasinski is going up against incumbent Sen. Vicki Jensen, a moderate Democrat and former Owatonna School Board Member who runs an insurance agency in the district.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; Terri Bonoff is on to bigger things this election cycle, making a run at the 3rd Congressional District and incumbent GOP U.S. Rep. Erik Paulsen. That leaves open this competitive Minnetonka and Plymouth-area seat, which Bonoff first won in a special election in 2005. She held on to the swing seat over the years with a moderate tone and business-friendly positions on everything from tax increases to the minimum wage. To replace Bonoff, Democrats recruited Deb Calvert, a former staffer for Sen. Amy Klobuchar who now works in the dean’s office at Mitchell Hamline School of Law. But Republicans are excited about their candidate, too: Paul Anderson, a well-known Republican activist who previously served as a deputy chief of staff for former Gov. Tim Pawlenty. As of now, this race on track to be the most expensive single race for the Legislature this year, with more than $700,000 spent by mid October.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; In the Senate, Republican Minority Leader David Hann holds the seat and has for the last 15 years. That’s not stopping Democrats from targeting his seat this fall with recruit Steve Cwodzinski, a popular American Government teacher in Eden Prairie High School who retired earlier this year. In particular, Democrats hope Trump’s unpopularity in the suburbs and support for a DFL-backed Southwest Light Rail Transit project will tip the race over into their control.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; Paul Marquart holds a unique distinction: He’s the only Democratic House member still serving in a district that also voted for Mitt Romney in 2012. House Republicans picked up all of the other remaining Romney-DFL districts in 2014, which put an immediate target on Marquart’s back this year. Marquart has survived plenty of tough races in the past, but Republicans are excited about Ben Grimsley, a financial advisor from Detroit lakes who recently picked up a critical newspaper endorsement from the Fargo Forum.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;h4&gt;State House District 5B&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Tom Anzelc (DFL)&lt;/span&gt; (First elected: 2006)&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Challengers:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-gp"&gt;Dennis Barsness (GP)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Sandy Layman (R)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;Last cycle, Republicans were not able to chip away at DFL Rep. Tom Anzelc's popularity in his Grand Rapids-area district. He easily held onto his seat in a tough year for the DFL. This year, they’ve recruited a well-known candidate in Sandy Layman, a former head of the Iron Range Resources and Rehabilitation Board (IRRRB). On top of that, Green Party candidate Dennis Barsness is also seeking the seat, and Republicans hope he ultimately siphons off votes from Anzelc. Republicans also say there could be a “Trump factor” in the district, as the wild card Republican nominee’s message seems to play well with disgruntled working-class voters in northern Minnesota.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;h4&gt;State House District 12A&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Jeff Backer (R)&lt;/span&gt; (First elected: 2014)&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Challengers:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Jay McNamar (DFL)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;Democrats are setting up a handful of rematches in the battle for the House this year, and that includes a do-over between GOP Rep. Jeff Backer and former DFL Rep. Jay McNamar. McNamar lost by about 700 votes in 2014, but Democrats point to his strong ties to the community. McNamara was an Elbow Lake fireman for 20 years and served as the city’s mayor. Republicans want to maintain the ground the picked up in rural Minnesota in 2014, making this a key race to watch.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;h4&gt;State House District 14A&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Tama Theis (R)&lt;/span&gt; (First elected: 2013)&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Challengers:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Aric Putnam (DFL)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;All three races in St. Cloud area are competitive this fall, with a surprising amount of cash flowing into Rep. Tama Theis’ side of the district. It leans more conservative than the other half of the district and hasn’t been targeted in the past, but this cycle it’s in the top ten most expensive House races. Democrats think the district, which includes part of St. Cloud, Waite Park and St. Augusta, is trending blue. Her challenger is Aric Putnam, a professor at St. John's University and the College of St. Benedict.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;h4&gt;State House District 14B&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Jim Knoblach (R)&lt;/span&gt; (First elected: 1994)&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Challengers:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Zachary &#034;Zach&#034; Dorholt (DFL)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;In St. Cloud Minnesota, incumbent GOP Rep. Jim Knoblach is in a close race that could cost House Republicans their chief budget guru. After previously serving in the House in the 1990s and early 2000s, Knoblach was re-elected to the chamber by just 60 votes votes in 2014. His previous experience in the House prompted Republicans, also newly in the majority, to place him as the powerful Ways and Means Committee chairman. But his opponent in 2014, former DFL Rep. Zachary Dorholt, is running again, and this year he’ll have boosted voter turnout at his back, especially in a town with a large public university. Expect Republicans to work hard to defend Knoblach, making this one of the most-watched — and the single most expensive — races in the House this cycle.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;h4&gt;State House District 17B&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Dave Baker (R)&lt;/span&gt; (First elected: 2014)&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Challengers:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Mary Sawatzky (DFL)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;The race for a Willmar-area House seat is another rematch from 2014. Two years ago, Republican businessman and political newcomer Dave Baker beat incumbent DFL Rep. Mary Sawatzky by less than one percentage point. This year, Sawatzky is hoping to win her seat back with much higher turnout. A teacher, Sawatzky will talk about bringing the same education opportunities metro students get to rural communities. Baker stood out in his first-term as a lawmaker willing to work across party lines, authoring legislation to require doctors and physicians to track patients and what opioids they are being prescribed.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="component-label"&gt;Map &amp; Total spending&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-container"&gt; &lt;div id="map-17b" class="map" style="height:400px;width:95%"&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;td class="text-right"&gt;$273,175.92&lt;/td&gt; &lt;/tr&gt; &lt;/tbody&gt; &lt;/table&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;&lt;small&gt;6th highest spending of all House districts.&lt;/small&gt;&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="past-result column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="component-label"&gt;Past election results&lt;/div&gt; &lt;table&gt; &lt;tbody&gt; &lt;tr&gt; &lt;td colspan="2"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;This district, 2014:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; He was a moderate Republican with a reputation for breaking away from his caucus, easily winning re-election after his first victory in 2008. But with his retirement from the Legislature this year, Democrats see an opportunity: The district voted for Obama over Romney in 2012, and they’ve recruited Lisa Bayley, a member of the Red Wing City Council for the last eight years. But Republicans are pretty happy with their recruit, too: Barb Haley, a lifelong Red Wing resident and businesswoman who volunteers in the community.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;h4&gt;State House District 24B&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Brian Daniels (R)&lt;/span&gt; (First elected: 2014)&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Challengers:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Patti Fritz (DFL)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;Patti Fritz served for a decade as a moderate Democrat in her conservative-leaning Faribault House district before she was knocked off in the GOP rural wave of 2014 by Republican newcomer Brian Daniels. Daniels has a record now, after serving one term in St. Paul, and Democrats hope they can use it to bring Fritz back to St. Paul. In particular, Fritz will talk about gridlock that blocked a transportation and bonding package from passing this spring.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; Not only was she the first woman ever represent the district, she was also the first Democrat elected in the northern Rochester seat in decades. But she’s retiring this fall, and the big question is how much has Rochester changed since Norton was first elected. Many think it’s become more reliably Democratic since then, but Republicans want to put that assumption to the test. They’ve recruited former GOP Rep. Fran Bradley, who held the seat before Norton in 2004. &lt;h4&gt;State House District 28B&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Gregory M. Davids (R)&lt;/span&gt; (First elected: 1991)&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Challengers:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Thomas Trehus (DFL)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;Twelve-term Republican Rep. Greg Davids has been a target of Democrats before, but it’s been awhile since he’s seen so much outside spending in his district. As of the late October campaign finance deadline, the race for his southeastern House seat is the third most expensive in the lower chamber. This cycle, Democrats like the contrast between Davids, chair of the House Taxes Committee, and Thomas Trehus, a young candidate who serves on a local school board and moved back to the district to live on his family farm.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="component-label"&gt;Map &amp; Total spending&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-container"&gt; &lt;div id="map-28b" class="map" style="height:400px;width:95%"&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;table&gt; &lt;tbody&gt; &lt;tr&gt; &lt;td&gt;Spent to help Democrats&lt;/td&gt; &lt;td class="text-right"&gt;$463,180.06&lt;/td&gt; &lt;/tr&gt; &lt;tr&gt; &lt;td&gt;Spent to help Republicans&lt;/td&gt; &lt;td class="text-right"&gt;$233,481.73&lt;/td&gt; &lt;/tr&gt; &lt;/tbody&gt; &lt;/table&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;&lt;small&gt;3rd highest spending of all House districts.&lt;/small&gt;&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="past-result column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="component-label"&gt;Past election results&lt;/div&gt; &lt;table&gt; &lt;tbody&gt; &lt;tr&gt; &lt;td colspan="2"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;This district, 2014:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; &lt;/tr&gt; &lt;tr&gt; &lt;td&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Gregory M. Sarah Anderson has fended off plenty of challenges from Democrats in her Plymouth-area house district over the years, but this time they’re taking her on with an intense, late-in the game spending blitz, one that suggests they see more openings in Minnesota’s suburbs this cycle. Anderson has denounced Trump and said she doesn’t plan to vote for him this cycle after the “Access Hollywood” comments were released. Her challenger, Ginny Klevorn, is a mediator who lives in Plymouth and volunteers at the area church.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; No matter what the electoral situation is — a presidential year or an off year — the race is always decided by just a handful of votes. DFL Rep. Yvonne Selcer won it by just 1 percentage point back in 2012 and held on to it by even less in 2014 — 41 votes. With Selcer retiring this year, it makes the seat even more competitive. Republican Mary Shapiro, a former Minneapolis School teacher, will go up against Laurie Pryor, a longtime DFL activist in the district.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Ron Erhardt (DFL)&lt;/span&gt; (First elected: 1990)&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Challengers:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Dario Anselmo (R)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;The race for an Edina seat in the state House features a rematch, DFL Rep. Ron Erhardt versus Republican challenger Dario Anselmo. In 2014, Anselmo came within 3 points of knocking off Erhardt. Conventional wisdom would suggest the incumbent will hold onto his seat in presidential year, when turnout is higher, but Republicans have launched an effective ad campaign against him. They’ve spent tens of thousands of dollars on a television ad and mailers featuring a woman who was part of an effort in 2015 to expand the state’s sexual assault laws to include bodily fluids after a co-worker ejaculated into her coffee cup. While legislators debated the issue on the House floor, &lt;a href="http://minnesota.cbslocal.com/2016/10/20/political-ad-joke-sex-assault-debate/" target="_blank"&gt;Erhardt joked and licked a coffee cup as he addressed other lawmakers.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Chad Anderson (R)&lt;/span&gt; (First elected: 2016)&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Challengers:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Andrew Carlson (DFL)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;The Bloomington House seat was long held by Democrats until February, when Republican newcomer Chad Anderson beat Bloomington City Council Member Andrew Carlson in a special election. That election was a sprint with a fair amount of outside campaigning from Republicans, who highlighted a vote on the council from Carlson to impose new regulations on garbage collection in Bloomington. Republicans said the victory was a sign they can play in Minnesota’s suburbs this fall, but Democrats expect it was a low-turnout special election fluke and, on paper, this looks a clear pick up opportunity for Democrats.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;h4&gt;State House District 54B&lt;/h4&gt; &lt;div class="current-race column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="to-watch"&gt;&lt;i class="fa fa-star" aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/i&gt;District to Watch&lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Incumbent:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li&gt;None - open seat&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Candidates:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;ul class="candidate-list"&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-dfl"&gt;Don Slaten (DFL)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li class="candidate"&gt;&lt;span class="color-political-r"&gt;Tony Jurgens (R)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;Republican Rep. Denny McNamara’s retirement from his eastern suburban House seat has sent both sides rushing to the district with resources. Republicans want to hold on to the swing district, especially as they cede more ground in the suburbs. Democrats see it as a possible pick up, and they’ve recruited candidate Don Slaten, a youth coach and member of a local machinist union. Republicans are running Tony Jurgens, an insurance agent living in the district.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;div class="component-label"&gt;Map &amp; &lt;/tr&gt; &lt;/tbody&gt; &lt;/table&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;&lt;small&gt;13th highest spending of all House districts.&lt;/small&gt;&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="past-result column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;The retirement of four-term Republican Rep. Tara Mack has turned her Apple Valley House District into one of the most competitive races in the 2016 cycle. Keith Ellison staffer Erin Maye Quade to run against Republican Ali Jimenez-Hopper, who grew up and raised her family in the district.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/div&gt; &lt;div class="map-demographics column-large-33 column-medium-50"&gt;
